[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your current candidates, ask the one follow-up question that best separates them.\n\n[Current Turn]\n11\n\n[Current Candidates]\nF50.8, F50.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, doctor, I guess it’s the eating… I mean, I’ve always cooked, you know, big pots of things, that’s how I was raised, and I still make those comfort foods even though it’s just me sometimes. But what worries me is once I start, I’ll tell myself, “Maria, that’s enough now,” and then my hand just goes right back to the pot anyway. It feels like I can’t make myself stop.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're in control when you're eating these large amounts of food?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, I start out thinking I’m just going to have a little, just taste it, you know, especially if I made one of those old comfort dishes like my mother used to make. And then I’ll tell myself, Maria, that’s enough now… but my hand just keeps going back to the pot. It’s like I know I should stop, but I can’t quite make myself do it.\n\nDoctor: How often do these episodes of eating more than you intended happen?\n\nPatient: Since my friend passed, it’s been most weeks, I’d say… usually once or twice. It’s often those quiet afternoons, after choir or when I’ve been out in the garden. I come in and the house is so still, and I’ll just start nibbling, and then it turns into more than I meant.\n\nDoctor: How do you feel about yourself after one of these episodes of eating more than you intended?\n\nPatient: Oh… afterward I feel just awful, honestly. Ashamed, mostly. Sad too. It’s silly, because those foods used to be about family, you know, my mother stirring a pot and everyone coming by, people laughing in the kitchen… and now it feels like I’ve turned it into something I’m hiding. Like if the ladies at church knew, I’d just want to disappear.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty specifically about how much you've eaten during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I do. It’s the amount, I think, that makes me feel so guilty afterward. I sit there and think, Lord, Maria, why did you eat all that? And then I feel ashamed and sad, because those foods used to mean family and taking care of people, you know… Sunday dinners and church ladies bringing casseroles. Now it feels like something I have to hide from them.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're eating very quickly during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yes… that’s the strange part. I’ve always been a slow eater, you know, the kind of person who takes forever at the table while everyone else is already clearing plates. My husband used to tease me about it.\n\nBut lately, when this happens, I’m eating fast… usually standing right there by the counter. I’ll take one bite and before I’ve even really tasted it, the next bite is already in my mouth. It’s not like how I used to eat at family meals at all.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over the past few months?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, doctor. I mean, my clothes feel different some days, but then I think, well, maybe it’s just the dryer, or the elastic going bad—you know how they don’t make things like they used to. I don’t weigh myself much anymore. The scale’s in the bathroom, but I try not to fuss with it too much. So… I can’t say I’ve noticed a big change, no.\n\nDoctor: Do you often think about these episodes of eating afterwards and criticize yourself for them?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. Afterward I’ll sit there and go over it in my head, and I’m not kind to myself about it.\n\nI feel ashamed, mostly… and sad. It’s like I took something that used to mean family and caring—feeding people, church suppers, bringing a dish when someone was poorly—and turned it into something secret. Something I wouldn’t want the ladies at church knowing about. That’s what hurts.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever try to make up for these episodes of eating by doing something to counteract the calories?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I quite understand what you mean by “make up for it.” Like… I don’t have some set thing I do afterward, if that’s what you’re asking.\n\nSometimes I’ll tell myself I should be better the next day, or I’ll fuss around the kitchen cleaning up—my mother always said you could tell a woman’s state of mind by her sink, which I don’t know if that’s true or just something people said back then. But as far as doing something specific to counter it… I don’t really know. I haven’t thought of it that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever try to purge or vomit after one of these episodes of eating?\n\nPatient: I don’t… I don’t think so. I mean, I’ve felt upset afterward, sure, and sometimes my stomach feels miserable if I’ve overdone it, but I haven’t sat there trying to make myself throw up.\n\nMy sister used to say when we were girls that ginger ale fixed everything, which of course it didn’t, but that’s the sort of thing I think of more than… vomiting on purpose. So no, I don’t believe I do that.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Exercise more… no, I don’t think that’s really me. I mean, I might tell myself I ought to move around more, but then I get sidetracked with the laundry or checking whether I left something out on the counter.

I used to walk more when my knees were better—my neighbor and I would go around the block and she’d point out every rosebush like she was giving a tour—but after one of those eating times, I can’t say I go out and exercise extra on purpose. I haven’t noticed doing that.

I mean, I might tell myself I ought to move around more, but then I get sidetracked with the laundry or checking whether I left something out on the counter.\n\nI used to walk more when my knees were better—my neighbor and I would go around the block and she’d point out every rosebush like she was giving a tour—but after one of those eating times, I can’t say I go out and exercise extra on purpose. I haven’t noticed doing that.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n12\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.8, F50.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, doctor, I guess it’s the eating… I mean, I’ve always cooked, you know, big pots of things, that’s how I was raised, and I still make those comfort foods even though it’s just me sometimes. But what worries me is once I start, I’ll tell myself, “Maria, that’s enough now,” and then my hand just goes right back to the pot anyway. It feels like I can’t make myself stop.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're in control when you're eating these large amounts of food?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, I start out thinking I’m just going to have a little, just taste it, you know, especially if I made one of those old comfort dishes like my mother used to make. And then I’ll tell myself, Maria, that’s enough now… but my hand just keeps going back to the pot. It’s like I know I should stop, but I can’t quite make myself do it.\n\nDoctor: How often do these episodes of eating more than you intended happen?\n\nPatient: Since my friend passed, it’s been most weeks, I’d say… usually once or twice. It’s often those quiet afternoons, after choir or when I’ve been out in the garden. I come in and the house is so still, and I’ll just start nibbling, and then it turns into more than I meant.\n\nDoctor: How do you feel about yourself after one of these episodes of eating more than you intended?\n\nPatient: Oh… afterward I feel just awful, honestly. Ashamed, mostly. Sad too. It’s silly, because those foods used to be about family, you know, my mother stirring a pot and everyone coming by, people laughing in the kitchen… and now it feels like I’ve turned it into something I’m hiding. Like if the ladies at church knew, I’d just want to disappear.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty specifically about how much you've eaten during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I do. It’s the amount, I think, that makes me feel so guilty afterward. I sit there and think, Lord, Maria, why did you eat all that? And then I feel ashamed and sad, because those foods used to mean family and taking care of people, you know… Sunday dinners and church ladies bringing casseroles. Now it feels like something I have to hide from them.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're eating very quickly during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yes… that’s the strange part. I’ve always been a slow eater, you know, the kind of person who takes forever at the table while everyone else is already clearing plates. My husband used to tease me about it.\n\nBut lately, when this happens, I’m eating fast… usually standing right there by the counter. I’ll take one bite and before I’ve even really tasted it, the next bite is already in my mouth. It’s not like how I used to eat at family meals at all.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over the past few months?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, doctor. I mean, my clothes feel different some days, but then I think, well, maybe it’s just the dryer, or the elastic going bad—you know how they don’t make things like they used to. I don’t weigh myself much anymore. The scale’s in the bathroom, but I try not to fuss with it too much. So… I can’t say I’ve noticed a big change, no.\n\nDoctor: Do you often think about these episodes of eating afterwards and criticize yourself for them?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. Afterward I’ll sit there and go over it in my head, and I’m not kind to myself about it.\n\nI feel ashamed, mostly… and sad. It’s like I took something that used to mean family and caring—feeding people, church suppers, bringing a dish when someone was poorly—and turned it into something secret. Something I wouldn’t want the ladies at church knowing about. That’s what hurts.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever try to make up for these episodes of eating by doing something to counteract the calories?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I quite understand what you mean by “make up for it.” Like… I don’t have some set thing I do afterward, if that’s what you’re asking.\n\nSometimes I’ll tell myself I should be better the next day, or I’ll fuss around the kitchen cleaning up—my mother always said you could tell a woman’s state of mind by her sink, which I don’t know if that’s true or just something people said back then. But as far as doing something specific to counter it… I don’t really know. I haven’t thought of it that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever try to purge or vomit after one of these episodes of eating?\n\nPatient: I don’t… I don’t think so. I mean, I’ve felt upset afterward, sure, and sometimes my stomach feels miserable if I’ve overdone it, but I haven’t sat there trying to make myself throw up.\n\nMy sister used to say when we were girls that ginger ale fixed everything, which of course it didn’t, but that’s the sort of thing I think of more than… vomiting on purpose. So no, I don’t believe I do that.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever exercise more than usual after one of these episodes of eating?\n\nPatient: Exercise more… no, I don’t think that’s really me. I mean, I might tell myself I ought to move around more, but then I get sidetracked with the laundry or checking whether I left something out on the counter.\n\nI used to walk more when my knees were better—my neighbor and I would go around the block and she’d point out every rosebush like she was giving a tour—but after one of those eating times, I can’t say I go out and exercise extra on purpose. I haven’t noticed doing that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty because you couldn't stop eating once you started?\n\nPatient: Yes… guilty, ashamed, really. Afterward I feel sad, like I’ve taken something that used to mean family and taking care of people—food, Sunday dinners, bringing a casserole to someone from church—and turned it into something I’m hiding.\n\nIt’s a funny thing, not funny ha-ha, but… I’ll think about the ladies at church setting out cookies on paper plates, everyone chatting, and then I feel like I’m not part of that anymore, like I’ve made it into something secret and ugly. So yes, I do feel bad because I couldn’t seem to stop.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I haven’t noticed doing that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty because you couldn't stop eating once you started?\n\nPatient: Yes… guilty, ashamed, really. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, doctor, I guess it’s the eating… I mean, I’ve always cooked, you know, big pots of things, that’s how I was raised, and I still make those comfort foods even though it’s just me sometimes. But what worries me is once I start, I’ll tell myself, “Maria, that’s enough now,” and then my hand just goes right back to the pot anyway. It feels like I can’t make myself stop.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're in control when you're eating these large amounts of food?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, I start out thinking I’m just going to have a little, just taste it, you know, especially if I made one of those old comfort dishes like my mother used to make. And then I’ll tell myself, Maria, that’s enough now… but my hand just keeps going back to the pot. It’s like I know I should stop, but I can’t quite make myself do it.\n\nDoctor: How often do these episodes of eating more than you intended happen?\n\nPatient: Since my friend passed, it’s been most weeks, I’d say… usually once or twice. It’s often those quiet afternoons, after choir or when I’ve been out in the garden. I come in and the house is so still, and I’ll just start nibbling, and then it turns into more than I meant.\n\nDoctor: How do you feel about yourself after one of these episodes of eating more than you intended?\n\nPatient: Oh… afterward I feel just awful, honestly. Ashamed, mostly. Sad too. It’s silly, because those foods used to be about family, you know, my mother stirring a pot and everyone coming by, people laughing in the kitchen… and now it feels like I’ve turned it into something I’m hiding. Like if the ladies at church knew, I’d just want to disappear.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty specifically about how much you've eaten during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I do. It’s the amount, I think, that makes me feel so guilty afterward. I sit there and think, Lord, Maria, why did you eat all that? And then I feel ashamed and sad, because those foods used to mean family and taking care of people, you know… Sunday dinners and church ladies bringing casseroles. Now it feels like something I have to hide from them.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're eating very quickly during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yes… that’s the strange part. I’ve always been a slow eater, you know, the kind of person who takes forever at the table while everyone else is already clearing plates. My husband used to tease me about it.\n\nBut lately, when this happens, I’m eating fast… usually standing right there by the counter. I’ll take one bite and before I’ve even really tasted it, the next bite is already in my mouth. It’s not like how I used to eat at family meals at all.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over the past few months?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, doctor. I mean, my clothes feel different some days, but then I think, well, maybe it’s just the dryer, or the elastic going bad—you know how they don’t make things like they used to. I don’t weigh myself much anymore. The scale’s in the bathroom, but I try not to fuss with it too much. So… I can’t say I’ve noticed a big change, no.\n\nDoctor: Do you often think about these episodes of eating afterwards and criticize yourself for them?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. Afterward I’ll sit there and go over it in my head, and I’m not kind to myself about it.\n\nI feel ashamed, mostly… and sad. It’s like I took something that used to mean family and caring—feeding people, church suppers, bringing a dish when someone was poorly—and turned it into something secret. Something I wouldn’t want the ladies at church knowing about. That’s what hurts.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever try to make up for these episodes of eating by doing something to counteract the calories?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I quite understand what you mean by “make up for it.” Like… I don’t have some set thing I do afterward, if that’s what you’re asking.\n\nSometimes I’ll tell myself I should be better the next day, or I’ll fuss around the kitchen cleaning up—my mother always said you could tell a woman’s state of mind by her sink, which I don’t know if that’s true or just something people said back then. But as far as doing something specific to counter it… I don’t really know. I haven’t thought of it that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever try to purge or vomit after one of these episodes of eating?\n\nPatient: I don’t… I don’t think so. So no, I don’t believe I do that.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever exercise more than usual after one of these episodes of eating?\n\nPatient: Exercise more… no, I don’t think that’s really me. I mean, I might tell myself I ought to move around more, but then I get sidetracked with the laundry or checking whether I left something out on the counter.\n\nI used to walk more when my knees were better—my neighbor and I would go around the block and she’d point out every rosebush like she was giving a tour—but after one of those eating times, I can’t say I go out and exercise extra on purpose. I haven’t noticed doing that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty because you couldn't stop eating once you started?\n\nPatient: Yes… guilty, ashamed, really. Afterward I feel sad, like I’ve taken something that used to mean family and taking care of people—food, Sunday dinners, bringing a casserole to someone from church—and turned it into something I’m hiding.\n\nIt’s a funny thing, not funny ha-ha, but… I’ll think about the ladies at church setting out cookies on paper plates, everyone chatting, and then I feel like I’m not part of that anymore, like I’ve made it into something secret and ugly. Afterward I feel sad, like I’ve taken something that used to mean family and taking care of people—food, Sunday dinners, bringing a casserole to someone from church—and turned it into something I’m hiding.\n\nIt’s a funny thing, not funny ha-ha, but… I’ll think about the ladies at church setting out cookies on paper plates, everyone chatting, and then I feel like I’m not part of that anymore, like I’ve made it into something secret and ugly. So yes, I do feel bad because I couldn’t seem to stop.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any periods of time where you've lost a significant amount of weight after these episodes of eating?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’ve noticed that, no. My weight’s gone this way and that over the years, but I can’t say there’s been some big drop after those eating times.\n\nI did have a skirt once that suddenly fit better, but then I think that was after I’d been busy cleaning out the hall closet—my daughter kept telling me I had linens in there from the Carter administration—so I don’t know if that’s the sort of thing you mean. But no, not a significant weight loss that I’ve tied to it.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you usually eat a large amount of food in a short time period during these episodes?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. Yes… I suppose it is a lot, when I’m honest about it. Some afternoons, like after choir practice or if I’ve been out fussing in the garden, I’ll make myself a plate of rice and beans, or maybe chicken stew if there’s some left.

And then I don’t just stop with the plate. I go back again… and then maybe again. I’ll tell myself I’m just evening it out in the container, you know, making room in the fridge, which is silly. Before I know it, I’ve eaten what really would’ve been two or three meals’ worth.

But what worries me is once I start, I’ll tell myself, “Maria, that’s enough now,” and then my hand just goes right back to the pot anyway. It feels like I can’t make myself stop.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're in control when you're eating these large amounts of food?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, I start out thinking I’m just going to have a little, just taste it, you know, especially if I made one of those old comfort dishes like my mother used to make. And then I’ll tell myself, Maria, that’s enough now… but my hand just keeps going back to the pot. It’s like I know I should stop, but I can’t quite make myself do it.\n\nDoctor: How often do these episodes of eating more than you intended happen?\n\nPatient: Since my friend passed, it’s been most weeks, I’d say… usually once or twice. It’s often those quiet afternoons, after choir or when I’ve been out in the garden. I come in and the house is so still, and I’ll just start nibbling, and then it turns into more than I meant.\n\nDoctor: How do you feel about yourself after one of these episodes of eating more than you intended?\n\nPatient: Oh… afterward I feel just awful, honestly. Ashamed, mostly. Sad too. It’s silly, because those foods used to be about family, you know, my mother stirring a pot and everyone coming by, people laughing in the kitchen… and now it feels like I’ve turned it into something I’m hiding. Like if the ladies at church knew, I’d just want to disappear.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty specifically about how much you've eaten during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I do. It’s the amount, I think, that makes me feel so guilty afterward. I sit there and think, Lord, Maria, why did you eat all that? And then I feel ashamed and sad, because those foods used to mean family and taking care of people, you know… Sunday dinners and church ladies bringing casseroles. Now it feels like something I have to hide from them.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're eating very quickly during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yes… that’s the strange part. I’ve always been a slow eater, you know, the kind of person who takes forever at the table while everyone else is already clearing plates. My husband used to tease me about it.\n\nBut lately, when this happens, I’m eating fast… usually standing right there by the counter. I’ll take one bite and before I’ve even really tasted it, the next bite is already in my mouth. It’s not like how I used to eat at family meals at all.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over the past few months?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, doctor. Afterward I’ll sit there and go over it in my head, and I’m not kind to myself about it.\n\nI feel ashamed, mostly… and sad. Something I wouldn’t want the ladies at church knowing about. But as far as doing something specific to counter it… I don’t really know. I haven’t thought of it that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever try to purge or vomit after one of these episodes of eating?\n\nPatient: I don’t… I don’t think so. My weight’s gone this way and that over the years, but I can’t say there’s been some big drop after those eating times.\n\nI did have a skirt once that suddenly fit better, but then I think that was after I’d been busy cleaning out the hall closet—my daughter kept telling me I had linens in there from the Carter administration—so I don’t know if that’s the sort of thing you mean. But no, not a significant weight loss that I’ve tied to it.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually eat a large amount of food in a short time period during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose it is a lot, when I’m honest about it. Some afternoons, like after choir practice or if I’ve been out fussing in the garden, I’ll make myself a plate of rice and beans, or maybe chicken stew if there’s some left.\n\nAnd then I don’t just stop with the plate. I go back again… and then maybe again. I’ll tell myself I’m just evening it out in the container, you know, making room in the fridge, which is silly. Before I know it, I’ve eaten what really would’ve been two or three meals’ worth.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you ever used laxatives or diuretics after one of these episodes of eating?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. Do NOT force an unrelated item.\n- On your first reply of the consultation, always return the item whose key is your\n  chief_complaint_symptom_code, whatever the doctor opened with.\n- Do not answer the doctor, and do not write anything outside the JSON.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"key\": \"<key>\", \"name\": \"<name>\", \"description\": \"<description>\", \"reason\": \"<why this item>\"}\n"
  },

I’m not sure… I don’t think so. Laxatives, you mean like the things people take when they’re stopped up? I can’t say I’ve made a habit of that after eating.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, doctor, I guess it’s the eating… I mean, I’ve always cooked, you know, big pots of things, that’s how I was raised, and I still make those comfort foods even though it’s just me sometimes. But what worries me is once I start, I’ll tell myself, “Maria, that’s enough now,” and then my hand just goes right back to the pot anyway. It feels like I can’t make myself stop.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're in control when you're eating these large amounts of food?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, I start out thinking I’m just going to have a little, just taste it, you know, especially if I made one of those old comfort dishes like my mother used to make. And then I’ll tell myself, Maria, that’s enough now… but my hand just keeps going back to the pot. It’s like I know I should stop, but I can’t quite make myself do it.\n\nDoctor: How often do these episodes of eating more than you intended happen?\n\nPatient: Since my friend passed, it’s been most weeks, I’d say… usually once or twice. It’s often those quiet afternoons, after choir or when I’ve been out in the garden. I come in and the house is so still, and I’ll just start nibbling, and then it turns into more than I meant.\n\nDoctor: How do you feel about yourself after one of these episodes of eating more than you intended?\n\nPatient: Oh… afterward I feel just awful, honestly. Ashamed, mostly. Sad too. It’s silly, because those foods used to be about family, you know, my mother stirring a pot and everyone coming by, people laughing in the kitchen… and now it feels like I’ve turned it into something I’m hiding. Like if the ladies at church knew, I’d just want to disappear.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty specifically about how much you've eaten during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I do. It’s the amount, I think, that makes me feel so guilty afterward. I sit there and think, Lord, Maria, why did you eat all that? And then I feel ashamed and sad, because those foods used to mean family and taking care of people, you know… Sunday dinners and church ladies bringing casseroles. Now it feels like something I have to hide from them.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're eating very quickly during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yes… that’s the strange part. I’ve always been a slow eater, you know, the kind of person who takes forever at the table while everyone else is already clearing plates. My husband used to tease me about it.\n\nBut lately, when this happens, I’m eating fast… usually standing right there by the counter. I’ll take one bite and before I’ve even really tasted it, the next bite is already in my mouth. It’s not like how I used to eat at family meals at all.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over the past few months?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, doctor. I mean, my clothes feel different some days, but then I think, well, maybe it’s just the dryer, or the elastic going bad—you know how they don’t make things like they used to. I don’t weigh myself much anymore. The scale’s in the bathroom, but I try not to fuss with it too much. So… I can’t say I’ve noticed a big change, no.\n\nDoctor: Do you often think about these episodes of eating afterwards and criticize yourself for them?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. Afterward I’ll sit there and go over it in my head, and I’m not kind to myself about it.\n\nI feel ashamed, mostly… and sad. It’s like I took something that used to mean family and caring—feeding people, church suppers, bringing a dish when someone was poorly—and turned it into something secret. Something I wouldn’t want the ladies at church knowing about. That’s what hurts.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever try to make up for these episodes of eating by doing something to counteract the calories?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I quite understand what you mean by “make up for it.” Like… I don’t have some set thing I do afterward, if that’s what you’re asking.\n\nSometimes I’ll tell myself I should be better the next day, or I’ll fuss around the kitchen cleaning up—my mother always said you could tell a woman’s state of mind by her sink, which I don’t know if that’s true or just something people said back then. But as far as doing something specific to counter it… I don’t really know. I haven’t thought of it that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever try to purge or vomit after one of these episodes of eating?\n\nPatient: I don’t… I don’t think so. I mean, I’ve felt upset afterward, sure, and sometimes my stomach feels miserable if I’ve overdone it, but I haven’t sat there trying to make myself throw up.\n\nMy sister used to say when we were girls that ginger ale fixed everything, which of course it didn’t, but that’s the sort of thing I think of more than… vomiting on purpose. So no, I don’t believe I do that.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever exercise more than usual after one of these episodes of eating?\n\nPatient: Exercise more… no, I don’t think that’s really me. I mean, I might tell myself I ought to move around more, but then I get sidetracked with the laundry or checking whether I left something out on the counter.\n\nI used to walk more when my knees were better—my neighbor and I would go around the block and she’d point out every rosebush like she was giving a tour—but after one of those eating times, I can’t say I go out and exercise extra on purpose. I haven’t noticed doing that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty because you couldn't stop eating once you started?\n\nPatient: Yes… guilty, ashamed, really. Afterward I feel sad, like I’ve taken something that used to mean family and taking care of people—food, Sunday dinners, bringing a casserole to someone from church—and turned it into something I’m hiding.\n\nIt’s a funny thing, not funny ha-ha, but… I’ll think about the ladies at church setting out cookies on paper plates, everyone chatting, and then I feel like I’m not part of that anymore, like I’ve made it into something secret and ugly. So yes, I do feel bad because I couldn’t seem to stop.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any periods of time where you've lost a significant amount of weight after these episodes of eating?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’ve noticed that, no. My weight’s gone this way and that over the years, but I can’t say there’s been some big drop after those eating times.\n\nI did have a skirt once that suddenly fit better, but then I think that was after I’d been busy cleaning out the hall closet—my daughter kept telling me I had linens in there from the Carter administration—so I don’t know if that’s the sort of thing you mean. But no, not a significant weight loss that I’ve tied to it.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually eat a large amount of food in a short time period during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose it is a lot, when I’m honest about it. Before I know it, I’ve eaten what really would’ve been two or three meals’ worth.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever used laxatives or diuretics after one of these episodes of eating?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I don’t think so. Laxatives, you mean like the things people take when they’re stopped up? don’t know that I’ve noticed that, no. But for me, after those times, mostly I just feel bad and try to put the kitchen back in order. Yes… I think that’s the part that gets me most. I’ll be standing there with the pot still on the stove, and I’ll say to myself, Maria, that is enough now. And then my hand just… goes right back in anyway.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, doctor, I guess it’s the eating… I mean, I’ve always cooked, you know, big pots of things, that’s how I was raised, and I still make those comfort foods even though it’s just me sometimes. But what worries me is once I start, I’ll tell myself, “Maria, that’s enough now,” and then my hand just goes right back to the pot anyway. It feels like I can’t make myself stop.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're in control when you're eating these large amounts of food?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, I start out thinking I’m just going to have a little, just taste it, you know, especially if I made one of those old comfort dishes like my mother used to make. And then I’ll tell myself, Maria, that’s enough now… but my hand just keeps going back to the pot. It’s like I know I should stop, but I can’t quite make myself do it.\n\nDoctor: How often do these episodes of eating more than you intended happen?\n\nPatient: Since my friend passed, it’s been most weeks, I’d say… usually once or twice. It’s often those quiet afternoons, after choir or when I’ve been out in the garden. I come in and the house is so still, and I’ll just start nibbling, and then it turns into more than I meant.\n\nDoctor: How do you feel about yourself after one of these episodes of eating more than you intended?\n\nPatient: Oh… afterward I feel just awful, honestly. Ashamed, mostly. Sad too. It’s silly, because those foods used to be about family, you know, my mother stirring a pot and everyone coming by, people laughing in the kitchen… and now it feels like I’ve turned it into something I’m hiding. Like if the ladies at church knew, I’d just want to disappear.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty specifically about how much you've eaten during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I do. It’s the amount, I think, that makes me feel so guilty afterward. I sit there and think, Lord, Maria, why did you eat all that? And then I feel ashamed and sad, because those foods used to mean family and taking care of people, you know… Sunday dinners and church ladies bringing casseroles. Now it feels like something I have to hide from them.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're eating very quickly during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yes… that’s the strange part. I’ve always been a slow eater, you know, the kind of person who takes forever at the table while everyone else is already clearing plates. My husband used to tease me about it.\n\nBut lately, when this happens, I’m eating fast… usually standing right there by the counter. I’ll take one bite and before I’ve even really tasted it, the next bite is already in my mouth. It’s not like how I used to eat at family meals at all.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over the past few months?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, doctor. I mean, my clothes feel different some days, but then I think, well, maybe it’s just the dryer, or the elastic going bad—you know how they don’t make things like they used to. I don’t weigh myself much anymore. The scale’s in the bathroom, but I try not to fuss with it too much. So… I can’t say I’ve noticed a big change, no.\n\nDoctor: Do you often think about these episodes of eating afterwards and criticize yourself for them?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. Afterward I’ll sit there and go over it in my head, and I’m not kind to myself about it.\n\nI feel ashamed, mostly… and sad. It’s like I took something that used to mean family and caring—feeding people, church suppers, bringing a dish when someone was poorly—and turned it into something secret. Something I wouldn’t want the ladies at church knowing about. That’s what hurts.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever try to make up for these episodes of eating by doing something to counteract the calories?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I quite understand what you mean by “make up for it.” Like… I don’t have some set thing I do afterward, if that’s what you’re asking.\n\nSometimes I’ll tell myself I should be better the next day, or I’ll fuss around the kitchen cleaning up—my mother always said you could tell a woman’s state of mind by her sink, which I don’t know if that’s true or just something people said back then. But as far as doing something specific to counter it… I don’t really know. I haven’t thought of it that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever try to purge or vomit after one of these episodes of eating?\n\nPatient: I don’t… I don’t think so. I mean, I’ve felt upset afterward, sure, and sometimes my stomach feels miserable if I’ve overdone it, but I haven’t sat there trying to make myself throw up.\n\nMy sister used to say when we were girls that ginger ale fixed everything, which of course it didn’t, but that’s the sort of thing I think of more than… vomiting on purpose. So no, I don’t believe I do that.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever exercise more than usual after one of these episodes of eating?\n\nPatient: Exercise more… no, I don’t think that’s really me. I mean, I might tell myself I ought to move around more, but then I get sidetracked with the laundry or checking whether I left something out on the counter.\n\nI used to walk more when my knees were better—my neighbor and I would go around the block and she’d point out every rosebush like she was giving a tour—but after one of those eating times, I can’t say I go out and exercise extra on purpose. I haven’t noticed doing that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty because you couldn't stop eating once you started?\n\nPatient: Yes… guilty, ashamed, really. Afterward I feel sad, like I’ve taken something that used to mean family and taking care of people—food, Sunday dinners, bringing a casserole to someone from church—and turned it into something I’m hiding.\n\nIt’s a funny thing, not funny ha-ha, but… I’ll think about the ladies at church setting out cookies on paper plates, everyone chatting, and then I feel like I’m not part of that anymore, like I’ve made it into something secret and ugly. So yes, I do feel bad because I couldn’t seem to stop.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any periods of time where you've lost a significant amount of weight after these episodes of eating?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’ve noticed that, no. My weight’s gone this way and that over the years, but I can’t say there’s been some big drop after those eating times.\n\nI did have a skirt once that suddenly fit better, but then I think that was after I’d been busy cleaning out the hall closet—my daughter kept telling me I had linens in there from the Carter administration—so I don’t know if that’s the sort of thing you mean. keep going back for more until I’ve eaten enough for two or three meals.\n- key: S050\n  name: Loss_of_Control\n  description: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n  manifestation: What brings me in is that once I start eating those comfort foods, I tell myself, 'Maria, that is enough,' but my hand still goes back to the pot like I can’t make myself stop.\n- key: S051\n  name: Rapid_Eating\n  description: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n  manifestation: I have always been a slow eater, but lately when this happens I eat fast standing by the counter, hardly tasting the food before the next bite is already in my mouth.\n- key: S052\n  name: Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full\n  description: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n  manifestation: By the time I stop, my stomach feels tight and heavy, and I have to sit in my recliner with my waistband loosened because I went past what felt comfortable.\n- key: S055\n  name: Negative_Affect_After_Binge\n  description: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n  manifestation: Afterward I feel ashamed and sad, like I turned something that used to mean family and caring into something I have to hide from people at church.\n- key: additional_requirement_1\n  name: Additional Requirement 1\n  description: The binge eating occurs, on average, at least once a week for 3 months\n  manifestation: Since my friend passed, this has been happening most weeks, usually once or twice in those quiet afternoons after choir or gardening.\n- key: functional_impairment\n  name: Functional Impairment\n  manifestation: I still go to Mass, choir, and take care of my home, but after one of these afternoons I may stay in and avoid calling anyone because I feel embarrassed.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 3 months\n  manifestation: This started after my close friend died, and it has been going on for about three months now.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 78 / not_in_workforce / 9th_12th_no_diploma\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S050\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Do you usually eat a large amount of food in a short time period during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose it is a lot, when I’m honest about it. Some afternoons, like after choir practice or if I’ve been out fussing in the garden, I’ll make myself a plate of rice and beans, or maybe chicken stew if there’s some left.\n\nAnd then I don’t just stop with the plate. I go back again… and then maybe again. I’ll tell myself I’m just evening it out in the container, you know, making room in the fridge, which is silly. Before I know it, I’ve eaten what really would’ve been two or three meals’ worth.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever used laxatives or diuretics after one of these episodes of eating?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I don’t think so. Laxatives, you mean like the things people take when they’re stopped up? I can’t say I’ve made a habit of that after eating.\n\nMy sister used to keep all sorts of things in her medicine cabinet, little bottles lined up like a pharmacy, and I always thought, goodness, how do you even keep track. But for me, after those times, mostly I just feel bad and try to put the kitchen back in order. I haven’t noticed using water pills or laxatives for that, no.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're criticizing yourself mostly because you couldn't control how much you ate?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s the part that gets me most. I’ll be standing there with the pot still on the stove, and I’ll say to myself, Maria, that is enough now. And then my hand just… goes right back in anyway.\n\nIt reminds me of when I used to tell the children not to pick at the chicken before supper, and there I am doing the same kind of thing at my age. But yes, it’s that feeling that I couldn’t make myself stop. That’s what I end up scolding myself about.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you usually feel uncomfortably full after one of these episodes of eating?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… yes, I do. By the time I finally stop, my stomach feels so tight and heavy, like I’ve packed it too full.

Sometimes I have to go sit in my recliner and loosen my waistband a bit. I hate admitting that, but it’s true. I’ll sit there thinking, why did I go past the point where it was still comfortable? And then I get distracted by the television or the cat making noise by the window, but the feeling’s still there… that overfull, uncomfortable feeling.

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n17\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.8, F50.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, doctor, I guess it’s the eating… I mean, I’ve always cooked, you know, big pots of things, that’s how I was raised, and I still make those comfort foods even though it’s just me sometimes. But what worries me is once I start, I’ll tell myself, “Maria, that’s enough now,” and then my hand just goes right back to the pot anyway. It feels like I can’t make myself stop.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're in control when you're eating these large amounts of food?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, I start out thinking I’m just going to have a little, just taste it, you know, especially if I made one of those old comfort dishes like my mother used to make. And then I’ll tell myself, Maria, that’s enough now… but my hand just keeps going back to the pot. It’s like I know I should stop, but I can’t quite make myself do it.\n\nDoctor: How often do these episodes of eating more than you intended happen?\n\nPatient: Since my friend passed, it’s been most weeks, I’d say… usually once or twice. It’s often those quiet afternoons, after choir or when I’ve been out in the garden. I come in and the house is so still, and I’ll just start nibbling, and then it turns into more than I meant.\n\nDoctor: How do you feel about yourself after one of these episodes of eating more than you intended?\n\nPatient: Oh… afterward I feel just awful, honestly. Ashamed, mostly. Sad too. It’s silly, because those foods used to be about family, you know, my mother stirring a pot and everyone coming by, people laughing in the kitchen… and now it feels like I’ve turned it into something I’m hiding. Like if the ladies at church knew, I’d just want to disappear.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty specifically about how much you've eaten during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I do. It’s the amount, I think, that makes me feel so guilty afterward. I sit there and think, Lord, Maria, why did you eat all that? And then I feel ashamed and sad, because those foods used to mean family and taking care of people, you know… Sunday dinners and church ladies bringing casseroles. Now it feels like something I have to hide from them.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're eating very quickly during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yes… that’s the strange part. I’ve always been a slow eater, you know, the kind of person who takes forever at the table while everyone else is already clearing plates. My husband used to tease me about it.\n\nBut lately, when this happens, I’m eating fast… usually standing right there by the counter. I’ll take one bite and before I’ve even really tasted it, the next bite is already in my mouth. It’s not like how I used to eat at family meals at all.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over the past few months?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, doctor. I mean, my clothes feel different some days, but then I think, well, maybe it’s just the dryer, or the elastic going bad—you know how they don’t make things like they used to. I don’t weigh myself much anymore. The scale’s in the bathroom, but I try not to fuss with it too much. So… I can’t say I’ve noticed a big change, no.\n\nDoctor: Do you often think about these episodes of eating afterwards and criticize yourself for them?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. Afterward I’ll sit there and go over it in my head, and I’m not kind to myself about it.\n\nI feel ashamed, mostly… and sad. It’s like I took something that used to mean family and caring—feeding people, church suppers, bringing a dish when someone was poorly—and turned it into something secret. Something I wouldn’t want the ladies at church knowing about. That’s what hurts.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever try to make up for these episodes of eating by doing something to counteract the calories?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I quite understand what you mean by “make up for it.” Like… I don’t have some set thing I do afterward, if that’s what you’re asking.\n\nSometimes I’ll tell myself I should be better the next day, or I’ll fuss around the kitchen cleaning up—my mother always said you could tell a woman’s state of mind by her sink, which I don’t know if that’s true or just something people said back then. But as far as doing something specific to counter it… I don’t really know. I haven’t thought of it that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever try to purge or vomit after one of these episodes of eating?\n\nPatient: I don’t… I don’t think so. I mean, I’ve felt upset afterward, sure, and sometimes my stomach feels miserable if I’ve overdone it, but I haven’t sat there trying to make myself throw up.\n\nMy sister used to say when we were girls that ginger ale fixed everything, which of course it didn’t, but that’s the sort of thing I think of more than… vomiting on purpose. So no, I don’t believe I do that.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever exercise more than usual after one of these episodes of eating?\n\nPatient: Exercise more… no, I don’t think that’s really me. I mean, I might tell myself I ought to move around more, but then I get sidetracked with the laundry or checking whether I left something out on the counter.\n\nI used to walk more when my knees were better—my neighbor and I would go around the block and she’d point out every rosebush like she was giving a tour—but after one of those eating times, I can’t say I go out and exercise extra on purpose. I haven’t noticed doing that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty because you couldn't stop eating once you started?\n\nPatient: Yes… guilty, ashamed, really. Afterward I feel sad, like I’ve taken something that used to mean family and taking care of people—food, Sunday dinners, bringing a casserole to someone from church—and turned it into something I’m hiding.\n\nIt’s a funny thing, not funny ha-ha, but… I’ll think about the ladies at church setting out cookies on paper plates, everyone chatting, and then I feel like I’m not part of that anymore, like I’ve made it into something secret and ugly. So yes, I do feel bad because I couldn’t seem to stop.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any periods of time where you've lost a significant amount of weight after these episodes of eating?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’ve noticed that, no. My weight’s gone this way and that over the years, but I can’t say there’s been some big drop after those eating times.\n\nI did have a skirt once that suddenly fit better, but then I think that was after I’d been busy cleaning out the hall closet—my daughter kept telling me I had linens in there from the Carter administration—so I don’t know if that’s the sort of thing you mean. But no, not a significant weight loss that I’ve tied to it.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually eat a large amount of food in a short time period during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose it is a lot, when I’m honest about it. Some afternoons, like after choir practice or if I’ve been out fussing in the garden, I’ll make myself a plate of rice and beans, or maybe chicken stew if there’s some left.\n\nAnd then I don’t just stop with the plate. I go back again… and then maybe again. I’ll tell myself I’m just evening it out in the container, you know, making room in the fridge, which is silly. Before I know it, I’ve eaten what really would’ve been two or three meals’ worth.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever used laxatives or diuretics after one of these episodes of eating?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I don’t think so. Laxatives, you mean like the things people take when they’re stopped up? I can’t say I’ve made a habit of that after eating.\n\nMy sister used to keep all sorts of things in her medicine cabinet, little bottles lined up like a pharmacy, and I always thought, goodness, how do you even keep track. But for me, after those times, mostly I just feel bad and try to put the kitchen back in order. I haven’t noticed using water pills or laxatives for that, no.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're criticizing yourself mostly because you couldn't control how much you ate?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s the part that gets me most. I’ll be standing there with the pot still on the stove, and I’ll say to myself, Maria, that is enough now. And then my hand just… goes right back in anyway.\n\nIt reminds me of when I used to tell the children not to pick at the chicken before supper, and there I am doing the same kind of thing at my age. But yes, it’s that feeling that I couldn’t make myself stop. That’s what I end up scolding myself about.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel uncomfortably full after one of these episodes of eating?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I do. By the time I finally stop, my stomach feels so tight and heavy, like I’ve packed it too full.\n\nSometimes I have to go sit in my recliner and loosen my waistband a bit. I hate admitting that, but it’s true. I’ll sit there thinking, why did I go past the point where it was still comfortable? And then I get distracted by the television or the cat making noise by the window, but the feeling’s still there… that overfull, uncomfortable feeling.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

I haven’t noticed using water pills or laxatives for that, no.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're criticizing yourself mostly because you couldn't control how much you ate?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s the part that gets me most. I’ll be standing there with the pot still on the stove, and I’ll say to myself, Maria, that is enough now. And then my hand just… goes right back in anyway.\n\nIt reminds me of when I used to tell the children not to pick at the chicken before supper, and there I am doing the same kind of thing at my age. But yes, it’s that feeling that I couldn’t make myself stop. That’s what I end up scolding myself about.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel uncomfortably full after one of these episodes of eating?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I do. By the time I finally stop, my stomach feels so tight and heavy, like I’ve packed it too full.\n\nSometimes I have to go sit in my recliner and loosen my waistband a bit. I hate admitting that, but it’s true. I’ll sit there thinking, why did I go past the point where it was still comfortable? And then I get distracted by the television or the cat making noise by the window, but the feeling’s still there… that overfull, uncomfortable feeling.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, doctor, I guess it’s the eating… I mean, I’ve always cooked, you know, big pots of things, that’s how I was raised, and I still make those comfort foods even though it’s just me sometimes. But what worries me is once I start, I’ll tell myself, “Maria, that’s enough now,” and then my hand just goes right back to the pot anyway. It feels like I can’t make myself stop.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're in control when you're eating these large amounts of food?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, I start out thinking I’m just going to have a little, just taste it, you know, especially if I made one of those old comfort dishes like my mother used to make. And then I’ll tell myself, Maria, that’s enough now… but my hand just keeps going back to the pot. It’s like I know I should stop, but I can’t quite make myself do it.\n\nDoctor: How often do these episodes of eating more than you intended happen?\n\nPatient: Since my friend passed, it’s been most weeks, I’d say… usually once or twice. It’s often those quiet afternoons, after choir or when I’ve been out in the garden. I come in and the house is so still, and I’ll just start nibbling, and then it turns into more than I meant.\n\nDoctor: How do you feel about yourself after one of these episodes of eating more than you intended?\n\nPatient: Oh… afterward I feel just awful, honestly. Ashamed, mostly. Sad too. It’s silly, because those foods used to be about family, you know, my mother stirring a pot and everyone coming by, people laughing in the kitchen… and now it feels like I’ve turned it into something I’m hiding. Like if the ladies at church knew, I’d just want to disappear.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty specifically about how much you've eaten during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I do. It’s the amount, I think, that makes me feel so guilty afterward. I sit there and think, Lord, Maria, why did you eat all that? And then I feel ashamed and sad, because those foods used to mean family and taking care of people, you know… Sunday dinners and church ladies bringing casseroles. Now it feels like something I have to hide from them.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're eating very quickly during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yes… that’s the strange part. I’ve always been a slow eater, you know, the kind of person who takes forever at the table while everyone else is already clearing plates. My husband used to tease me about it.\n\nBut lately, when this happens, I’m eating fast… usually standing right there by the counter. I’ll take one bite and before I’ve even really tasted it, the next bite is already in my mouth. It’s not like how I used to eat at family meals at all.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over the past few months?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, doctor. I mean, my clothes feel different some days, but then I think, well, maybe it’s just the dryer, or the elastic going bad—you know how they don’t make things like they used to. I don’t weigh myself much anymore. The scale’s in the bathroom, but I try not to fuss with it too much. So… I can’t say I’ve noticed a big change, no.\n\nDoctor: Do you often think about these episodes of eating afterwards and criticize yourself for them?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. Afterward I’ll sit there and go over it in my head, and I’m not kind to myself about it.\n\nI feel ashamed, mostly… and sad. It’s like I took something that used to mean family and caring—feeding people, church suppers, bringing a dish when someone was poorly—and turned it into something secret. Something I wouldn’t want the ladies at church knowing about. That’s what hurts.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever try to make up for these episodes of eating by doing something to counteract the calories?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I quite understand what you mean by “make up for it.” Like… I don’t have some set thing I do afterward, if that’s what you’re asking.\n\nSometimes I’ll tell myself I should be better the next day, or I’ll fuss around the kitchen cleaning up—my mother always said you could tell a woman’s state of mind by her sink, which I don’t know if that’s true or just something people said back then. But as far as doing something specific to counter it… I don’t really know. I haven’t thought of it that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever try to purge or vomit after one of these episodes of eating?\n\nPatient: I don’t… I don’t think so. I mean, I’ve felt upset afterward, sure, and sometimes my stomach feels miserable if I’ve overdone it, but I haven’t sat there trying to make myself throw up.\n\nMy sister used to say when we were girls that ginger ale fixed everything, which of course it didn’t, but that’s the sort of thing I think of more than… vomiting on purpose. So no, I don’t believe I do that.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever exercise more than usual after one of these episodes of eating?\n\nPatient: Exercise more… no, I don’t think that’s really me. I mean, I might tell myself I ought to move around more, but then I get sidetracked with the laundry or checking whether I left something out on the counter.\n\nI used to walk more when my knees were better—my neighbor and I would go around the block and she’d point out every rosebush like she was giving a tour—but after one of those eating times, I can’t say I go out and exercise extra on purpose. I haven’t noticed doing that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty because you couldn't stop eating once you started?\n\nPatient: Yes… guilty, ashamed, really. Afterward I feel sad, like I’ve taken something that used to mean family and taking care of people—food, Sunday dinners, bringing a casserole to someone from church—and turned it into something I’m hiding.\n\nIt’s a funny thing, not funny ha-ha, but… I’ll think about the ladies at church setting out cookies on paper plates, everyone chatting, and then I feel like I’m not part of that anymore, like I’ve made it into something secret and ugly. So yes, I do feel bad because I couldn’t seem to stop.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any periods of time where you've lost a significant amount of weight after these episodes of eating?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’ve noticed that, no. My weight’s gone this way and that over the years, but I can’t say there’s been some big drop after those eating times.\n\nI did have a skirt once that suddenly fit better, but then I think that was after I’d been busy cleaning out the hall closet—my daughter kept telling me I had linens in there from the Carter administration—so I don’t know if that’s the sort of thing you mean. But no, not a significant weight loss that I’ve tied to it.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually eat a large amount of food in a short time period during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose it is a lot, when I’m honest about it. Some afternoons, like after choir practice or if I’ve been out fussing in the garden, I’ll make myself a plate of rice and beans, or maybe chicken stew if there’s some left.\n\nAnd then I don’t just stop with the plate. I go back again… and then maybe again. I’ll tell myself I’m just evening it out in the container, you know, making room in the fridge, which is silly. Before I know it, I’ve eaten what really would’ve been two or three meals’ worth.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever used laxatives or diuretics after one of these episodes of eating?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I don’t think so. Now it feels like something I have to hide from them.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're eating very quickly during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yes… that’s the strange part. I’ve always been a slow eater, you know, the kind of person who takes forever at the table while everyone else is already clearing plates. My husband used to tease me about it.\n\nBut lately, when this happens, I’m eating fast… usually standing right there by the counter. I’ll take one bite and before I’ve even really tasted it, the next bite is already in my mouth. It’s not like how I used to eat at family meals at all.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over the past few months?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, doctor. I mean, my clothes feel different some days, but then I think, well, maybe it’s just the dryer, or the elastic going bad—you know how they don’t make things like they used to. I don’t weigh myself much anymore. The scale’s in the bathroom, but I try not to fuss with it too much. So… I can’t say I’ve noticed a big change, no.\n\nDoctor: Do you often think about these episodes of eating afterwards and criticize yourself for them?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. Afterward I’ll sit there and go over it in my head, and I’m not kind to myself about it.\n\nI feel ashamed, mostly… and sad. It’s like I took something that used to mean family and caring—feeding people, church suppers, bringing a dish when someone was poorly—and turned it into something secret. Something I wouldn’t want the ladies at church knowing about. That’s what hurts.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever try to make up for these episodes of eating by doing something to counteract the calories?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I quite understand what you mean by “make up for it.” Like… I don’t have some set thing I do afterward, if that’s what you’re asking.\n\nSometimes I’ll tell myself I should be better the next day, or I’ll fuss around the kitchen cleaning up—my mother always said you could tell a woman’s state of mind by her sink, which I don’t know if that’s true or just something people said back then. But as far as doing something specific to counter it… I don’t really know. I haven’t thought of it that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever try to purge or vomit after one of these episodes of eating?\n\nPatient: I don’t… I don’t think so. I mean, I’ve felt upset afterward, sure, and sometimes my stomach feels miserable if I’ve overdone it, but I haven’t sat there trying to make myself throw up.\n\nMy sister used to say when we were girls that ginger ale fixed everything, which of course it didn’t, but that’s the sort of thing I think of more than… vomiting on purpose. So no, I don’t believe I do that.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever exercise more than usual after one of these episodes of eating?\n\nPatient: Exercise more… no, I don’t think that’s really me. I mean, I might tell myself I ought to move around more, but then I get sidetracked with the laundry or checking whether I left something out on the counter.\n\nI used to walk more when my knees were better—my neighbor and I would go around the block and she’d point out every rosebush like she was giving a tour—but after one of those eating times, I can’t say I go out and exercise extra on purpose. I haven’t noticed doing that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty because you couldn't stop eating once you started?\n\nPatient: Yes… guilty, ashamed, really. Afterward I feel sad, like I’ve taken something that used to mean family and taking care of people—food, Sunday dinners, bringing a casserole to someone from church—and turned it into something I’m hiding.\n\nIt’s a funny thing, not funny ha-ha, but… I’ll think about the ladies at church setting out cookies on paper plates, everyone chatting, and then I feel like I’m not part of that anymore, like I’ve made it into something secret and ugly. So yes, I do feel bad because I couldn’t seem to stop.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any periods of time where you've lost a significant amount of weight after these episodes of eating?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’ve noticed that, no. My weight’s gone this way and that over the years, but I can’t say there’s been some big drop after those eating times.\n\nI did have a skirt once that suddenly fit better, but then I think that was after I’d been busy cleaning out the hall closet—my daughter kept telling me I had linens in there from the Carter administration—so I don’t know if that’s the sort of thing you mean. But no, not a significant weight loss that I’ve tied to it.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually eat a large amount of food in a short time period during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose it is a lot, when I’m honest about it. Some afternoons, like after choir practice or if I’ve been out fussing in the garden, I’ll make myself a plate of rice and beans, or maybe chicken stew if there’s some left.\n\nAnd then I don’t just stop with the plate. I go back again… and then maybe again. I’ll tell myself I’m just evening it out in the container, you know, making room in the fridge, which is silly. Before I know it, I’ve eaten what really would’ve been two or three meals’ worth.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever used laxatives or diuretics after one of these episodes of eating?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I don’t think so. Laxatives, you mean like the things people take when they’re stopped up? I can’t say I’ve made a habit of that after eating.\n\nMy sister used to keep all sorts of things in her medicine cabinet, little bottles lined up like a pharmacy, and I always thought, goodness, how do you even keep track. But for me, after those times, mostly I just feel bad and try to put the kitchen back in order. I haven’t noticed using water pills or laxatives for that, no.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're criticizing yourself mostly because you couldn't control how much you ate?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s the part that gets me most. I’ll be standing there with the pot still on the stove, and I’ll say to myself, Maria, that is enough now. And then my hand just… goes right back in anyway.\n\nIt reminds me of when I used to tell the children not to pick at the chicken before supper, and there I am doing the same kind of thing at my age. But yes, it’s that feeling that I couldn’t make myself stop. That’s what I end up scolding myself about.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel uncomfortably full after one of these episodes of eating?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I do. By the time I finally stop, my stomach feels so tight and heavy, like I’ve packed it too full.\n\nSometimes I have to go sit in my recliner and loosen my waistband a bit. I hate admitting that, but it’s true. I’ll sit there thinking, why did I go past the point where it was still comfortable? And then I get distracted by the television or the cat making noise by the window, but the feeling’s still there… that overfull, uncomfortable feeling.\n\nDoctor: Would you describe these episodes of eating as feeling like binge eating to you?\n\nPatient: I suppose… yes, that’s what it feels like to me, if I’m understanding the word right. It’s not just eating a little extra.\n\nWhat brings me in is that once I start with those comfort foods, I’ll tell myself, Maria, that is enough now, and then my hand still goes right back to the pot like I can’t quite make myself stop. And it’s silly, because I know better, I’ve cooked for a family for years, you know, I know what a serving is supposed to look like… but in that moment it doesn’t seem to matter. So yes, it feels like I lose control of it."
  }
]
